Quillen cohomology of enriched operads
Hoang Truong

Abstract
A modern insight due to Quillen, which is further developed by Lurie, asserts that many cohomology theories of interest are particular cases of a single construction, which allows one to define cohomology groups in an abstract setting using only intrinsic properties of the category (or ∞ \infty -category) at hand. This universal cohomology theory is known as Quillen cohomology. In any setting, Quillen cohomology of a given object is classified by its cotangent complex. The main purpose of this paper is to study Quillen cohomology of operads enriched over a general base category. Our main result provides an explicit formula for computing Quillen cohomology of enriched operads, based on a procedure of taking certain infinitesimal models of their cotangent complexes. Furthermore, we propose a natural construction of the twisted arrow ∞ \infty -categories of simplicial operads. We then assert that the cotangent complex of a simplicial operad can be represented as a spectrum valued functor on its twisted arrow ∞ \infty -category. When working in stable base categories such as chain complexes and spectra, Francis and Lurie proved the existence of a fiber sequence relating the cotangent complex and Hochschild complex of an E n \E_{n} -algebra, from which a conjecture of Kontsevich is verified. We establish an analogous fiber sequence for the operad E n \E_{n} itself, in the topological setting.



1
Introduction

1.1
An overview of the subject

A widespread idea in the domain of homotopy theory is to study a given object of interest by associating to it various kinds of cohomology groups. From generalized cohomology theories for spaces and various Ext groups in homological algebra, through group cohomology, sheaf cohomology, Hochschild cohomology and André-Quillen cohomology, such invariants vary from fairly useful to utterly indispensable. A modern insight due to Quillen
[
46
]
, which is further developed by Lurie
[
37
]
, asserts that all these cohomology theories are particular cases of a single universal construction, which allows one to define cohomology groups in an abstract setting using only intrinsic properties of the category (or
\infty
-category) at hand. This universal cohomology theory is known as
Quillen cohomology
.

In Quillen’s approach, cohomology of an object of interest is classified by its derived abelianization. Suppose we are given a model category
M
. Recall that an
abelian group object
in
M
is an object
A
equipped with two maps
*\longrightarrow A
and
A\times A\longrightarrow A
subject to the classical axioms of an abelian group. For each object
X\in\textbf{M}
, the category of abelian group objects in
\textbf{M}_{/X}
, denoted
\Ab(\textbf{M}_{/X})
, possibly inherits a model structure transferred from that of
M
. In this situation, the free-forgetful adjunction
\mathcal{F}:\textbf{M}_{/X}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\Ab(\textbf{M}_{/X}):\mathcal{U}
forms a Quillen adjunction. The
cotangent complex
of
X
, denoted by
\rL_{X}
, is then defined to be
\rL_{X}:=\mathbb{L}\mathcal{F}(X)
the
derived abelianization of

X
. Moreover, given an object
M\in\Ab(\textbf{M}_{/X})
, the
n
’th
Quillen cohomology group of
X
with coefficients in

M
is computed by the formula

\sH^{n}_{Q}(X;M)=\pi_{0}\Map^{\h}_{\Ab(\textbf{M}_{/X})}(\rL_{X},M[n])

where
M[n]
refers to the
n
-suspension of
M
in
\Ab(\textbf{M}_{/X})
. The work of Quillen was first devoted to establishing a proper cohomology theory for rings and commutative algebras (cf.
[
47
]
), which is nowadays generalized into the operadic context as described in the following example.

Example 1.1.1
.

Let
k
be a commutative ring that contains the field
\mathbb{Q}
of rational numbers, and let
\mathcal{P}
be an operad enriched over dg
k
-modules. For each
\mathcal{P}
-algebra
A
, the free-forgetful adjunction
(\Alg_{\mathcal{P}})_{/A}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\Ab((\Alg_{\mathcal{P}})_{/A})
is (homotopically) equivalent to the adjunction

\Omega^{A}(-):(\Alg_{\mathcal{P}})_{/A}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\Mod^{A}_{\mathcal{P}}:A\ltimes(-)

in which
\Mod^{A}_{\mathcal{P}}
refers to the category of
A
-modules over

\mathcal{P}
, the left adjoint sends
B\in(\Alg_{\mathcal{P}})_{/A}
to
\Omega^{A}(B)
the
module of Kähler differentials
of
B
over
A
, while the right adjoint sends
M\in\Mod^{A}_{\mathcal{P}}
to
A\ltimes M
the
square-zero extension
of
A
by
M
. Therefore, after sending coefficients into
\Mod^{A}_{\mathcal{P}}
, the
n
’th Quillen cohomology group of
A
with coefficients in an object
M\in\Mod^{A}_{\mathcal{P}}
is given by

\sH^{n}_{Q}(A;M)=\pi_{0}\Map^{\h}_{\Mod^{A}_{\mathcal{P}}}(\Omega^{A}(A^{\cof}),M[n])

where
A^{\cof}
is a cofibrant resolution of
A
in
\Alg_{\mathcal{P}}
. (See, e.g.,
[
34
,
39
,
22
]
).

Quillen’s approach has certain limitations, despite its success. Indeed, there is not a known criterion assuring the existence of the transferred model structure on abelian group objects in a given model category and moreover, even when realized, this model category structure is not invariant under Quillen equivalences. Improving the work of Quillen, Lurie (
[
37
]
) established the cotangent complex formalism, when working in the
\infty
-categorical framework. In his approach, the notion of derived abelianization is extended to that of
stabilization
, which itself is inspired by the classical theory of spectra. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a presentable
\infty
-category and let
X
be an object of
\mathcal{C}
. Consider the over
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}_{/X}
. Conceptually, the stabilization of
\mathcal{C}_{/X}
is given by the
\infty
-categorical limit of the tower

\cdots\overset{\Omega}{\longrightarrow}(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{*}\overset{\Omega}{\longrightarrow}(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{*}\overset{\Omega}{\longrightarrow}(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{*}

where
\Omega
refers to the desuspension functor on
(\mathcal{C}_{/X})_{*}
the pointed
\infty
-category associated to
\mathcal{C}_{/X}
. As in
[
37
]
, we will refer to the stabilization of
\mathcal{C}_{/X}
as the
tangent category to
\mathcal{C}
at
X
and denote it by
\mathcal{T}_{X}\mathcal{C}
. By construction,
\mathcal{T}_{X}\mathcal{C}
is automatically a
stable
\infty
-category
. Moreover, the presentability of
\mathcal{C}
implies that the canonical functor
\mathcal{T}_{X}\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{/X}
admits a left adjoint called
suspension spectrum functor
and denoted by
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}:\mathcal{C}_{/X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{X}\mathcal{C}
. By this way, the
cotangent complex of

X
is defined to be
\rL_{X}:=\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}(X)
. Moreover, the
n
’th Quillen cohomology group of
X
with coefficients in a given object
M\in\mathcal{T}_{X}\mathcal{C}
is now formulated as

\sH^{n}_{Q}(X;M):=\pi_{0}\Map_{\mathcal{T}_{X}\mathcal{C}}(\rL_{X},M[n]).

We refer the readers to
[
22
]
for a discussion on the naturality of the evolution from Quillen’s approach to Lurie’s, and a comparison between them also.

For necessary computations in abstract homotopy theory, model categories seem to be the most favorable environment. Fortunately, the Lurie’s setting mentioned above was completely translated into the model categorical language, thanks to the recent works of Y. Harpaz, J. Nuiten and M. Prasma
[
24
,
22
]
. Following the setting given in
[
24
]
, tangent (model) categories come after a procedure of taking left Bousfield localizations of model categories of interest. Nevertheless, the obstacle is that left Bousfield localizing usually requires the left properness. The recent result of Batanin-White
[
4
]
allows one to take left Bousfield localizations of
semi model categories
(cf., e.g.,
[
18
,
2
,
52
]
) without requiring the left properness. Inspired by this, under our setting, tangent categories exist as semi model categories, which are basically as convenient as (full) model categories.

The main purpose of this paper is to formulate Quillen cohomology of operads enriched over a general symmetric monoidal model category, which we will refer to as the
base category
. Given a base category
\mathcal{S}
, we denote by
\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
the category of
\mathcal{S}
-enriched
C
-colored operads
with
C
being some fixed set of colors, yet the one we really care about is the category of
\mathcal{S}
-enriched operads
(with non-fixed sets of colors), which will be denoted by
\Op(\mathcal{S})
. Under some suitable conditions,
\Op(\mathcal{S})
admits the
canonical model structure
, according to Caviglia’s
[
8
]
. In particular, when
\mathcal{S}
is the category
\Set_{\Delta}
of simplicial sets equipped with the standard model structure, the canonical model structure on
\Op(\Set_{\Delta})
agrees with the
Cisinski-Moerdijk model structure
, which was known to be a model for the theory of
\infty
-operads
(cf.
[
9
]
).

Given an
\mathcal{S}
-enriched
C
-colored operad
\mathcal{P}
, one can consider it either as an object of
\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
or as an object of
\Op(\mathcal{S})
. As mentioned, our main focus is on the latter case. Therefore, we will refer to the Quillen cohomology of
\mathcal{P}\in\Op(\mathcal{S})
as its
(proper) Quillen cohomology
. In contrast, the corresponding cohomology of
\mathcal{P}\in\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
is referred to as its
reduced Quillen cohomology
. Some attention was given in the literature to the reduced Quillen cohomology of operads. For instance, in the context of dg operads, this was studied by Loday-Merkulov-Vallette
[
34
,
39
]
, (in which the resultant is described in terms of derivations, similarly as in Example
1.1.1
). On the other hand, the problems of formulating Quillen cohomology of operads and investigating its applications have not yet been considered so far.

It has been widely acknowledged that Quillen cohomology theory keeps a key role in the study of
deformation theory
and
obstruction theory
. Let us discuss these in what follows.

Naively speaking, a deformation of an object of interest under
``
small perturbation” is an object of the same type which is
``
equivalent” to the original object. In our setting, a small perturbation is precisely an
artinian dg

k
-
algebra
with
k
being a field of characteristic
0
, i.e. a (connective) augmented commutative dg
k
-algebra
R
of finite dimension such that the augmentation map
R\rightarrow\textbf{k}
exhibits the
0
’th homology of
R
as a local
k
-algebra. For instance, in the context of algebraic objects (e.g., dg modules, dg categories and dg operads), a deformation of an object
X
under a small perturbation
R\rightarrow\textbf{k}
is by definition an object
Y
(parameterized over
R
) coming together with a weak equivalence
Y\otimes_{R}\textbf{k}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}X
. One can then organize all the deformations of
X
into a category such that every morphism is an equivalence of deformations. The
\infty
-groupoid associated to this category will be called the
space of deformations of
X
over

R
, denoted by
\Def(X,R)
. In a somewhat more abstract setting presented in a parallel paper (joint with Y. Harpaz), we propose the notion of spaces of deformations for various types of object. We then show that Quillen cohomology of a given object indeed classifies the homotopy type of its spaces of deformations. Moreover, we show that the functor
R\mapsto\Def(X,R)
forms a
formal moduli problem
in the sense of
[
36
]
. It implies that the deformations of
X
are
``
governed” by a single dg Lie algebra (cf.
[
36
,
45
]
).

For two topological spaces
X
and
Y
, understanding maps from
X
to
Y
(up to homotopy) is a classical problem in homotopy theory. Suppose that
Y
is simply connected. As the first step, one filters
Y
by its
Postnikov tower
:

\cdots\longrightarrow P_{n}(Y)\longrightarrow P_{n-1}(Y)\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow P_{1}(Y)\longrightarrow P_{0}(Y).

The problem is therefore reduced to understanding maps
X\longrightarrow P_{n}(Y)
that extend some given map
f:X\longrightarrow P_{n-1}(Y)
. It is known that the obstruction to a section
P_{n-1}(Y)\longrightarrow P_{n}(Y)
is classified by a single cohomology class
k_{n-1}\in\sH^{n+1}(P_{n-1}(Y);\pi_{n}Y)
. More generally, the obstruction to an extension
X\longrightarrow P_{n}(Y)
of
f
is classified by the image of
k_{n-1}
under the induced map

f^{*}:\sH^{n+1}(P_{n-1}(Y);\pi_{n}Y)\longrightarrow\sH^{n+1}(X;\pi_{n}Y).

The latter is called the
obstruction class of
f
. Note that the ordinary cohomology of spaces is nothing but a particular case of Quillen cohomology. The authors of
[
16
]
generalized the above machinery to study the obstruction theory of
simplicial categories
with fixed set of objects, in which case obstruction classes are indeed contained in Quillen cohomology groups. The obstruction theory of
dg categories
was considered in
[
53
]
, in which case Quillen cohomology plays a central role again. We believe that the present paper may open the way to the study of obstruction theory of simplicial operads.

1.2
Main statements

We now present a summary of our main results together with some historical background. We would like to divide this subsection into three parts.

1.2.1
Operadic tangent categories and cotangent complex of enriched operads.

Suppose we are given a sufficiently nice base category
\mathcal{S}
(cf. Conventions
3.0.2
). As the starting point, we extend a result of
[
22
]
, which we now recall. Let
\mathcal{C}\in\Cat(\mathcal{S})
be an
\mathcal{S}
-enriched category. We denote by
\Cat_{C}(\mathcal{S})
the category of
\mathcal{S}
-enriched categories that admit
C:=\Ob(\mathcal{C})
as the fixed set of objects, and denote by
\BMod(\mathcal{C})
the category of
\mathcal{C}
-bimodules
. There is a sequence of the obvious Quillen adjunctions

\BMod(\mathcal{C})_{\mathcal{C}/}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\Cat_{C}(\mathcal{S})_{\mathcal{C}/}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\Cat(\mathcal{S})_{\mathcal{C}/}.

Theorem 1.2.1
.

(Y. Harpaz, J. Nuiten and M. Prasma
[
22
]
) The above sequence induces a sequence of Quillen equivalences connecting the associated tangent categories:

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}\BMod(\mathcal{C})\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\simeq\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}\Cat_{C}(\mathcal{S})\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\simeq\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}\Cat(\mathcal{S})

(1.2.1)

We now fix
\mathcal{P}
to be a
\Sigma
-cofibrant
C
-colored operad in
\mathcal{S}
. We will let
\BMod(\mathcal{P})
and
\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
respectively denote the categories of
\mathcal{P}
-bimodules
and
infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodules
. Note that the structure of bimodules over an operad is not linear in general. The notion of infinitesimal bimodules comes naturally as the linearization of that usual notion of operadic bimodules, and in particular, is much more convenient for computations. Our creed is to exhibit the Quillen cohomology groups of an operad in terms of infinitesimal bimodules over it. We start with observing that there is a sequence of Quillen adjunctions

\IbMod(\mathcal{P})_{\mathcal{P}/}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\BMod(\mathcal{P})_{\mathcal{P}/}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})_{\mathcal{P}/}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\Op(\mathcal{S})_{\mathcal{P}/}

formed by the obvious induction-restriction adjunctions.

Theorem 1.2.2
.

(
4.4.1
,
4.4.3
) The above sequence induces a sequence of Quillen equivalences connecting the associated tangent categories:

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\IbMod(\mathcal{P})\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\simeq\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\BMod(\mathcal{P})\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\simeq\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\simeq\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\Op(\mathcal{S}).

Moreover, when
\mathcal{S}
is in addition stable containing a strict zero object, all the categories above are Quillen equivalent to
\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
.

To give a convenient formula for the Quillen cohomology of
\mathcal{P}
, we describe the derived image of the cotangent complex
\rL_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\Op(\mathcal{S})
under the right Quillen equivalence
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\Op(\mathcal{S})\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
. To this end, we first prove the following key proposition, which extends [
[
22
]
, Proposition 3.2.1]. We will assume further that
\mathcal{P}
is cofibrant, and is good in the sense of Definition
5.1.2
.

Proposition 1.2.3
.

(
5.2.5
) Under the Quillen equivalence
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\Op(\mathcal{S})\simeq\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\BMod(\mathcal{P})
, the cotangent complex
\rL_{\mathcal{P}}
is identified with
\rL^{b}_{\mathcal{P}}[-1]
where
\rL^{b}_{\mathcal{P}}
signifies the cotangent complex of
\mathcal{P}
considered as a bimodule over itself.

The object
\rL^{b}_{\mathcal{P}}
can be well calculated, so that we can get a nice description of its derived image in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
. We will denote the latter by
\widetilde{\rL}_{\mathcal{P}}
. Furthermore, we denote by
\sS^{n}
the
pointed
n
-sphere
in
\mathcal{S}
, and denote by
\sS_{C}^{n}
the
C
-collection which has
\sS_{C}^{n}(c;c)=\sS^{n}
for every
c\in C
and agrees with
\emptyset_{\mathcal{S}}
on the other levels.

Theorem 1.2.4
.

(
5.2.15
,
5.2.16
) The
n
’th Quillen cohomology group of
\mathcal{P}
with coefficients in a given object
M\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
is computed by the formula

\sH^{n}_{Q}(\mathcal{P};M)\cong\pi_{0}\Map^{\h}_{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\IbMod(\mathcal{P})}(\widetilde{\rL}_{\mathcal{P}},M[n+1])

in which
\widetilde{\rL}_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
is the prespectrum with
(\widetilde{\rL}_{\mathcal{P}})_{n,n}=\mathcal{P}\circ\sS_{C}^{n}
for
n\geqslant 0
. If
\mathcal{S}
is in addition stable and contains a strict zero object
0
then the
n
’th Quillen cohomology group of
\mathcal{P}
with coefficients in an object
M\in\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
is computed by

\sH^{n}_{Q}(\mathcal{P};M)\cong\pi_{0}\Map^{\h}_{\IbMod(\mathcal{P})}(\overline{\rL}_{\mathcal{P}},M[n+1])

where
\overline{\rL}_{\mathcal{P}}\in\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
is given at each
C
-sequence
\overline{c}:=(c_{1},\cdots,c_{m};c)
by

\overline{\rL}_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{c})=\mathcal{P}(\overline{c})\otimes\hocolim_{n}\Omega^{n}[\,(\sS^{n})^{\otimes m}\times^{\h}_{1_{\mathcal{S}}}0\,].

Besides that, we find a connection between Quillen cohomology and reduced Quillen cohomology of
\mathcal{P}
, expressed as follows.

Theorem 1.2.5
.

(
5.3.2
) Given an object
M\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
, there is a long exact sequence of abelian groups of the form

\cdots\longrightarrow\sH^{n-1}_{Q}(\mathcal{P};M)\longrightarrow\sH^{n}_{Q,r}(\mathcal{P};M)\longrightarrow\sH^{n}_{Q,\red}(\mathcal{P};M)\longrightarrow\sH^{n}_{Q}(\mathcal{P};M)\longrightarrow\sH^{n+1}_{Q,r}(\mathcal{P};M)\longrightarrow\cdots

where
\sH^{\bullet}_{Q,r}(\mathcal{P};-)
refers to Quillen cohomology group of
\mathcal{P}
when regarded as a right module over itself and
\sH^{\bullet}_{Q,\red}(\mathcal{P};-)
refers to reduced Quillen cohomology group of
\mathcal{P}
.

1.2.2
Twisted arrow
\infty
-categories and the cotangent complex of simplicial operads.

Our result concerning the cotangent complex of simplicial operads (or
\infty
-operads) will provide a generalization for the following.

Theorem 1.2.6
.

(Y. Harpaz, J. Nuiten and M. Prasma
[
22
]
) Let
\mathcal{C}
be a fibrant simplicial category. There is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}\Cat(\Set_{\Delta})_{\infty}\simeq\Fun(\Tw(\mathcal{C}),\Sp)

with
\Sp
being the
\infty
-category of spectra and
\Tw(\mathcal{C})
being the
twisted arrow

\infty
-
category
of
\mathcal{C}
. Furthermore, the cotangent complex
\rL_{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{C}}\Cat(\Set_{\Delta})
is then identified with the constant functor
\Tw(\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\Sp
on
\mathbb{S}[-1]
, i.e., the desuspension of the sphere spectrum. Consequently, the
n
’th Quillen cohomology group of
\mathcal{C}
with coefficients in a functor
\mathcal{F}:\Tw(\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\Sp
is given by

\sH^{n}_{Q}(\mathcal{C};\mathcal{F})=\pi_{-n-1}\lim\mathcal{F}.

The construction of twisted arrow
\infty
-categories (of
\infty
-categories)

\Tw(-):\Cat_{\infty}\longrightarrow\Cat_{\infty}

was originally introduced by Lurie [
[
37
]
,
\lx@sectionsign
5.2]. We extend that to the construction of
twisted arrow

\infty
-
categories of simplicial operads
. Let
\mathcal{P}
be a fibrant simplicial operad. Conceptually, the twisted arrow
\infty
-category
\Tw(\mathcal{P})
is defined to be the
covariant unstraightening
of the simplicial functor
\mathcal{P}:\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}\longrightarrow\Set_{\Delta}
, which encodes the data of
\mathcal{P}
as an infinitesimal bimodule over itself (see
\lx@sectionsign
2.2
). In particular, objects of
\Tw(\mathcal{P})
are precisely the
operations
of
\mathcal{P}
(i.e., the vertices of spaces of operations of
\mathcal{P}
), while the morphisms of
\Tw(\mathcal{P})
are given by the factorizations of operations. For examples,
\Tw(\Com)
is equivalent to
\Fin_{*}^{\op}
the (opposite) category of finite pointed sets, while
\Tw(\Ass)
is equivalent to the simplex category
\Delta
. Moreover, we also give a model for the twisted arrow
\infty
-category of the
little
n
-discs operad

\E_{n}
in
\lx@sectionsign
6.2
.

Theorem 1.2.7
.

(
6.3.3
) Let
\mathcal{P}
be a fibrant and
\Sigma
-cofibrant simplicial operad. Then there is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\Op(\Set_{\Delta})_{\infty}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Fun(\Tw(\mathcal{P}),\Sp).

Moreover, under this equivalence, the cotangent complex
\rL_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\Op(\Set_{\Delta})_{\infty}
is identified with the desuspension of the functor
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}}:\Tw(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow\Sp
given on objects by sending each operation
\mu\in\mathcal{P}
of arity
m
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mu)=\mathbb{S}^{\oplus m}
the
m
-fold coproduct of the sphere spectrum. Consequently, the
n
’th Quillen cohomology group of
\mathcal{P}
with coefficients in a given functor
\mathcal{F}:\Tw(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow\Sp
is given by the formula

\sH^{n}_{Q}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F})=\pi_{0}\Map_{\Fun(\Tw(\mathcal{P}),\Sp)}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{F}[n+1]).

For example, we consider the case where
\mathcal{P}=\E_{\infty}
. Denote by
\Mod_{\textbf{k}}
the category of modules over a given commutative ring
k
. A
right
\Gamma
-module
is by definition a functor
T:\Fin_{*}^{\op}\longrightarrow\Mod_{\textbf{k}}
. There is a significant invariant for right
\Gamma
-modules given by the
stable cohomotopy groups
(cf.
[
43
]
), which plays a key role in the
\E_{\infty}
-
obstruction theory
initiated by Robinson
[
50
]
. In Proposition
6.3.9
, we show that for each integer
n
there is an isomorphism

\pi^{n}(T)\cong\sH^{n-1}_{Q}(\E_{\infty};\widetilde{T})

between the
n
’th stable cohomotopy group of
T
and the
(n-1)
’th Quillen cohomology group of
\E_{\infty}
with coefficients in the induced functor
\widetilde{T}:\Tw(\E_{\infty})\simeq\sN(\Fin_{*}^{\op})\longrightarrow\Sp
. This observation reaffirms the fact that the obstruction for an
\E_{\infty}
-structure
is indeed controlled by the Quillen cohomology groups of
\E_{\infty}
(cf. [
[
50
]
, Theorem 5]).

1.2.3
Quillen principle and the cotangent complex of
\E_{n}
-operads.

In the context of algebraic objects, Quillen cohomology has attracted a lot of attention in literature because of its central role in the deformation theory. A traditional method for computing the
deformation complex
(or
tangent complex
) of a given object is to relate the cotangent complex to a
Hochschild-type complex
of that object via a nice enough fiber sequence. When such a fiber sequence exists, we will say that the object is subject to the
Quillen principle
. Let us recall some fundamental results based on that kind of thinking.

Let
k
be a commutative ring that contains the field
\mathbb{Q}
.

Example 1.2.8
.

At the beginning, for
A
a cofibrant
k
-algebra, Quillen
[
47
]
showed that there is a fiber sequence of
A
-bimodules of the form

\rL_{A}\longrightarrow A\otimes A\longrightarrow A

(1.2.2)

in which
A\otimes A
represents the free
A
-bimodule generated by
k
and the second map is induced by the unit map
\textbf{k}\longrightarrow A
. This tells us that the deformation complex of
A
can be described via the Hochschild cohomology of
A
, which is really accessible using the bar resolution.

Example 1.2.9
.

Lie algebras are subject to the Quillen principle as well. Indeed, for
\mathfrak{g}
a cofibrant Lie algebra over
k
, there is a fiber sequence of (left)
\mathfrak{g}
-modules:

\rL_{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow\sU(\mathfrak{g})\longrightarrow\textbf{k}

(1.2.3)

in which
\sU(\mathfrak{g})
is the universal enveloping algebra (regarded as the free
\mathfrak{g}
-module generated by
k
) and
k
is equipped with the trivial
\mathfrak{g}
-action. Due to the above fiber sequence, we may describe the deformation complex of
\mathfrak{g}
using the usual
Lie cohomology
of
\mathfrak{g}
, which can be viewed as a variant of the Hochschild-cohomology formalism.

Example 1.2.10
.

In fact, an analogue was observed for discrete groups even earlier. For a group
G
, one paid attention to the
augmentation ideal

I_{G}
fitting into the following exact sequence of
G
-modules:

I_{G}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[G]\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}

(1.2.4)

where
\mathbb{Z}
is equipped with the trivial
G
-action. It turns out that
I_{G}
is a model for the
Beck module
of
G
, which is nothing but the (underived) cotangent complex of
G
. A generalized version of (
1.2.4
) for
simplicial groups
can be found in
[
22
]
.

Furthermore, Kontsevich
[
31
]
predicted the existence of a fiber sequence relating the tangent complex and the Hochschild cohomology complex of a dg
\E_{n}
-algebra (up to a shift by
n
). A generalization of this conjecture was proved independently by Francis [
[
17
]
, Theorem 2.26] and Lurie [
[
37
]
, Theorem 7.3.5.1], when working in suitable stable base categories. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable presentable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category whose monoidal product distributes over colimits, and let
A
be an
\E_{n}
-algebra in
\mathcal{C}
. Denote by
\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})
the
\infty
-category of
A
-modules over
\E_{n}
. The authors first show that there is a composed equivalence of
\infty
-categories:

\mathcal{T}_{A}\Alg_{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{T}_{A}\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})\overset{\Psi_{A}}{\underset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}}\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C}).

(1.2.5)

We use the same notation
\rL_{A}
to denote the cotangent complex of
A\in\Alg_{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})
and also, its images in
\mathcal{T}_{A}\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})
and
\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})
. The authors verify a higher version of (
1.2.2
), meaning that the Quillen principle holds for
\E_{n}
-algebras, as indicated below.

Theorem 1.2.11
.

(Francis
[
17
]
, Lurie
[
37
]
) There is a fiber sequence in
\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})
of the form

\Free(\textbf{1})\overset{\eta_{A}}{\longrightarrow}A\longrightarrow\rL_{A}[n]

(1.2.6)

where
\Free
denotes the free functor
\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})
and
1
refers to the unit of
A
.

This indeed gives a solution for Kontsevich’s conjecture because the middle term of (
1.2.6
) represents the Hochschild complex of
A
itself.

Remark 1.2.12
.

It is natural to ask how an unstable-analogue of (
1.2.6
) (i.e. when the base category is no longer stable) can be formulated. Let us first discuss the sequence (
1.2.6
) further. Note that under the equivalence
\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})\simeq\mathcal{T}_{A}\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})
, the map
\eta_{A}:\Free(\textbf{1})\longrightarrow A
is identified with the map

\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}(\eta_{A})\longrightarrow\rL^{m}_{A}

where
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}(\eta_{A})
is the image of
\eta_{A}
under the functor
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}:\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})_{/A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{A}\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})
and
\rL^{m}_{A}
denotes the cotangent complex of
A
regarded as a module over itself. So the sequence (
1.2.6
) is identified with a fiber sequence in
\mathcal{T}_{A}\Mod_{A}^{\E_{n}}(\mathcal{C})
of the form

\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}(\eta_{A})\longrightarrow\rL^{m}_{A}\longrightarrow\rL_{A}[n]

(1.2.7)

Moreover, note that in the composed functor (
1.2.5
), the first functor is an equivalence even in general base categories, whilst
\Psi_{A}
is one due to the stability of
\mathcal{C}
. Therefore, we can say that an unstable-analogue of (
1.2.6
), if any, should take the form (
1.2.7
). Alternatively, it means
\rL_{A}[n]
is equivalent to
\rL_{A/\Free(\textbf{1})}
the
relative cotangent complex
of
\eta_{A}
(see Definition
2.5.13
).

A proof of the existence of (
1.2.7
) for topological
\E_{n}
-algebras can now be found in
[
28
]
, where we consider not only the cotangent complex of such objects but also algebras over a general enriched operad. In the present paper, it is more convenient for us to concentrate solely on the cotangent complex of topological
\E_{n}
-operads.

We assert that the cotangent complex of the operad
\E_{n}
is also subject to the Quillen principle. We denote by
\mu_{0}\in\E_{n}(0)
the unique nullary operation of
\E_{n}
regarded as an object of
\Tw(\E_{n})
. Furthermore, denote by
\eta_{!}(\mathbb{S})
the left Kan extension of the map
\{\mathbb{S}\}\longrightarrow\Sp
along the inclusion
\eta:\{\mu_{0}\}\longrightarrow\Tw(\E_{n})
.

Theorem 1.2.13
.

(
6.3.6
,
6.3.7
) There is a canonical fiber sequence in
\Fun(\Tw(\E_{n}),\Sp)
of the form

\eta_{!}(\mathbb{S})\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{S}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\E_{n}}[n],

(1.2.8)

where
\overline{\mathbb{S}}
denotes the constant functor on the sphere spectrum. Consequently, for a given functor
\mathcal{F}:\Tw(\E_{n})\longrightarrow\Sp
, there is a long exact sequence of abelian groups of the form

\cdots\longrightarrow\sH^{-k-n-1}_{Q}(\E_{n};\mathcal{F})\longrightarrow\pi_{k}\lim\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow\pi_{k}\mathcal{F}(\mu_{0})\longrightarrow\sH^{-k-n}_{Q}(\E_{n};\mathcal{F})\longrightarrow\pi_{k-1}\lim\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow\cdots.

In particular, when
\mathcal{F}(\mu_{0})=0
then for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have a canonical isomorphism

\sH^{-k-n}_{Q}(\E_{n};\mathcal{F})\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\pi_{k-1}\lim\mathcal{F}.

Remark 1.2.14
.

By Theorem
1.2.7
, the sequence (
1.2.8
) can be equivalently rewritten as

\eta_{!}(\mathbb{S})\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{S}}\longrightarrow\rL_{\E_{n}}[n+1]

(1.2.9)

Here we use the same notation for the image of the cotangent complex
\rL_{\E_{n}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{P}}\Op(\Set_{\Delta})_{\infty}
in
\Fun(\Tw(\E_{n}),\Sp)
. We denote by
\Free^{ib}(\mu_{0})
the free infinitesimal
\E_{n}
-bimodule generated by a single point (concentrated in level
0
), and write
\eta_{\E_{n}}:\Free^{ib}(\mu_{0})\longrightarrow\E_{n}
for the map classified by
\mu_{0}\in\E_{n}(0)
. We can show that under the equivalence of
\infty
-categories:

\Fun(\Tw(\E_{n}),\Sp)\simeq\mathcal{T}_{\E_{n}}\IbMod(\E_{n})_{\infty},

the map
\eta_{!}(\mathbb{S})\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{S}}
is identified with the canonical map
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}(\eta_{\E_{n}})\longrightarrow\rL^{ib}_{\E_{n}}
where
\rL^{ib}_{\E_{n}}
denotes the cotangent complex of
\E_{n}
regarded as an infinitesimal bimodule over itself. Thus, according to the logic as in Remark
1.2.12
, we will refer to (
1.2.8
) as the
Quillen principle for topological
\E_{n}
-operads
.

Remark 1.2.15
.

The Quillen principle for
\E_{n}
-operads in stable base categories, such as chain complexes over a commutative ring and symmetric spectra, becomes even more outstanding. Namely, in this case we may obtain a fiber sequence of infinitesimal
\E_{n}
-bimodules of the form

\Free^{ib}(\mu_{0})\overset{\eta_{\E_{n}}}{\longrightarrow}\E_{n}\longrightarrow\rL_{\E_{n}}[n+1]

(1.2.10)

Here, we maintain the same notation for the image of
\rL_{\E_{n}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\E_{n}}\Op(\mathcal{S})
under the equivalence
\mathcal{T}_{\E_{n}}\Op(\mathcal{S})\simeq\IbMod(\E_{n})
(see Theorem
1.2.2
). This verifies the operadic-analogue of Kontsevich’s conjecture because the middle term of (
1.2.10
) represents the Hochschild complex of
\E_{n}
itself. On a different aspect, applying the functor
(-)\circ_{\E_{n}}A
(cf. Example
1.3.2
) to (
1.2.10
), we may recover the fiber sequence (
1.2.6
) of Francis-Lurie. This reveals that Quillen cohomology of algebras over an operad can be controlled by Quillen cohomology of the operad itself. We will address these in a subsequent paper.

1.3
A preview on
\sT
-cohomology

We are giving a brief preview of what we would like to develop in future work. Let us first discuss the construction
\Tw(-)
a little more. We let
\textbf{S}:=(\Set_{\Delta})_{\infty}
denote the
\infty
-category of spaces
. Let
\mathcal{P}
be a fibrant simplicial operad (which, for simplicity, has a single color). For an infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule
M
, we can associate to it a functor
M^{\star}:\Tw(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow\textbf{S}
taking an operation
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(k)
to the space
M(k)
. As discussed in Remark
6.2.6
, we have a weak equivalence of spaces:

\lim_{\Tw(\mathcal{P})}M^{\star}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Map^{\h}_{\IbMod(\mathcal{P})}(\mathcal{P},M)

(1.3.1)

The story becomes more interesting when examining some special cases of
\mathcal{P}
and
M
. For the case of the little discs operads, it will be noteworthy that there is an
\infty
-categorical filtration of the nerve of
\Fin_{*}^{\op}
:

\sN(\Delta)\simeq\Tw(\E_{1})\longrightarrow\Tw(\E_{2})\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow\Tw(\E_{\infty})\simeq\sN(\Fin_{*}^{\op}).

Example 1.3.1
.

Let
f:\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
be a map of single-colored simplicial operads. Considering
\mathcal{Q}
as an infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule with the structure induced by
f
, we obtain a functor
\mathcal{Q}^{\star}:\Tw(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow\textbf{S}
. In particular, for the case where
\mathcal{P}=\E_{1}
, then
\mathcal{Q}^{\star}
is identified with a cosimplicial space, that was first considered by McClure-Smith
[
38
]
, and developed further in
[
14
]
with the aim of studying the space of maps
\E_{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
. More generally, for the case in which
\mathcal{P}=\E_{n}
for some
n<\infty
and
\mathcal{Q}
is such that
\mathcal{Q}(0)\simeq\mathcal{Q}(1)\simeq*
, then according to [
[
12
]
, Theorem 1.10], we have weak equivalences

\lim_{\Tw(\E_{n})}\mathcal{Q}^{\star}\,\simeq\,\Map^{\h}_{\IbMod(\E_{n})}(\E_{n},\mathcal{Q})\,\simeq\,\Omega^{n+1}\Map^{\h}_{\Op_{*}}(\E_{n},\mathcal{Q})

where
\Map^{\h}_{\Op_{*}}(-,-)
refers to the derived mapping space between simplicial single-colored operads, and the
(n+1)
-fold loop space is taken at the base point
f
.

Let us assume further that
\mathcal{P}
is
\Sigma
-cofibrant.

Example 1.3.2
.

Let
A\in\Alg_{\mathcal{P}}(\Set_{\Delta})
be a
\mathcal{P}
-algebra. For any
A
-module
N
, the
endomorphism
\Sigma_{*}
-object

\textrm{End}_{A,N}
is defined by, for each
k\geq 0
, letting

\textrm{End}_{A,N}(k):=\Map_{\Set_{\Delta}}(A^{\times\,k},N).

Moreover, this construction determines the right adjoint of a Quillen adjunction:

(-)\circ_{\mathcal{P}}A\,\colon\,\IbMod(\mathcal{P})\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr}}}\Mod_{\mathcal{P}}^{A}\penalty\mspace{6muplus1mu}{:}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu\mathpunct{}\mspace{2mu}\,\textrm{End}_{A,-}

in which the left adjoint sends each
M\in\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
to the relative composite product
M\circ_{\mathcal{P}}A
. (Notice
that in a general base category, it is necessary to require that
A
has a cofibrant underlying object). Thus
we have a chain of weak equivalences

\lim_{\Tw(\mathcal{P})}\textrm{End}_{A,N}^{\star}\,\simeq\,\Map^{\h}_{\IbMod(\mathcal{P})}(\mathcal{P},\textrm{End}_{A,N})\,\simeq\,\Map^{\h}_{\Mod_{\mathcal{P}}^{A}}(A,N),

provided further that
A\in\Alg_{\mathcal{P}}(\Set_{\Delta})
is cofibrant and
N\in\Mod_{\mathcal{P}}^{A}
is fibrant.

Now let
\mathcal{S}
be a symmetric monoidal model category equipped with a weak monoidal Quillen adjunction

\mathbb{F}\,\colon\,\Set_{\Delta}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr}}}\mathcal{S}\penalty\mspace{6muplus1mu}{:}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu\mathpunct{}\mspace{2mu}\,\mathbb{U}

(cf.
[
51
]
). The cases of interest will be
\Sp^{\Sigma}
the category of symmetric spectra, the category
\mathcal{C}(\textbf{k})
of chain complexes over a commutative ring
k
, and
\Set_{\Delta}
itself. For an infinitesimal
\mathbb{F}\mathcal{P}
-bimodule
M
, by adjunction we obtain a weak equivalence of the form

\lim_{\Tw(\mathcal{P})}(\mathbb{U}M)^{\star}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Map^{\h}_{\IbMod(\mathbb{F}\mathcal{P})}(\mathbb{F}\mathcal{P},M)

(1.3.2)

Here the images through
\mathbb{F}
and
\mathbb{U}
are assumed to be in the right homotopy type already.

Example 1.3.3
.

Let
A
be a cofibrant
\mathbb{F}\mathcal{P}
-algebra and let
N
be a fibrant
A
-module. Picking up the weak equivalences from Example
1.3.2
, we obtain a weak equivalence of the form

\lim_{\Tw(\mathcal{P})}(\mathbb{U}\textrm{End}_{A,N})^{\star}\,\simeq\,\Map^{\h}_{\Mod_{\mathbb{F}\mathcal{P}}^{A}}(A,N).

Due to this, when
\mathcal{P}=\Ass
and
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{C}(\textbf{k})
(resp.
\mathcal{S}=\Sp^{\Sigma}
), we recover the classical definition of Hochschild cohomology (resp. topological Hochschild cohomology). More generally, when
\mathcal{P}=\E_{n}
(for
n<\infty
), we deduce that the space
\lim_{\Tw(\mathcal{P})}(\mathbb{U}\textrm{End}_{A,N})^{\star}
represents the
\E_{n}
-Hochschild cohomology of
A
with coefficients in
N
.

Motivated by the observations above and by the results given in the previous subsection, we propose a cohomology theory for simplicial operads, for which we call
\sT
-cohomology
. Here the letter
``\sT
” stands for
``
twist”. This is because the definition of
\sT
-cohomology is based on the construction of twisted arrow
\infty
-categories of operads.

Definition 1.3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a fibrant and
\Sigma
-cofibrant simplicial operad. The
\sT
-
cohomology complex of

\mathcal{P}
with coefficients in a functor
\mathcal{F}:\Tw(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
is defined by

\HT^{\star}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{F}):=\lim\mathcal{F}.

Dually, the
\sT
-
homology complex of

\mathcal{P}
with coefficients in a functor
\mathcal{G}:\Tw(\mathcal{P})^{\op}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
is defined by

\HT_{\star}(\mathcal{P};\mathcal{G}):=\colim\mathcal{G}.

Remark 1.3.5
.

When
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}=\Sp
the
\infty
-category of spectra, then
\sT
-cohomology of
\mathcal{P}
coincides with Quillen cohomology of
\mathcal{P}
considered as an infinitesimal bimodule over itself (see Remark
1.2.14
); and additionally, for the case where
\mathcal{P}=\E_{n}
then
\sT
-cohomology is very close to Quillen cohomology as mentioned in Theorem
1.2.13
and Remark
1.2.15
.

Remark 1.3.6
.

Unfortunately,
\sT
-cohomology of the operad
\E_{\infty}
is not at all interesting because the category
\Tw(\E_{\infty})\simeq\sN(\Fin_{*}^{\op})
contains
\langle 0\rangle
as its zero object. Despite this, for a given functor
\mathcal{F}:\Tw(\E_{\infty})\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
, it would be interesting to consider
\sT
-cohomology of
\E_{n}
with coefficients in the restriction of
\mathcal{F}
to
\Tw(\E_{n})
, i.e. the composed functor

\Tw(\E_{n})\longrightarrow\Tw(\E_{\infty})\overset{\mathcal{F}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{S}_{\infty}.

For the base category of modules over a field of characteristic
0
, this type of (co)homology was first considered by Loday
[
33
]
, and developed further by Pirashvili
[
44
]
, in which the obtained (co)homology groups admit a
Hodge-type decomposition
(see loc. cit. for more details).

Remark 1.3.7
.

Notice also that when
\mathcal{P}
is concentrated in arity
1
(i.e.
\mathcal{P}
is identified with an
\infty
-category), then
\sT
-cohomology is just the usual cohomology of
\infty
-categories, which is a natural generalization of the
Baues-Wirsching cohomology
for small categories (cf.
[
5
]
). Furthermore, when
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}=\Sp
, then
\sT
-cohomology of
\mathcal{P}
is (up to a shift) equivalent to Quillen cohomology of
\mathcal{P}
, according to Theorem
1.2.6
of Harpaz-Nuiten-Prasma.

In summary, the spirit of the proposed cohomology theory can be outlined as follows:
\sT
-cohomology is established in the fashion of
functor cohomology
, and can be thought of as a kind of generalized Hochschild cohomology that is controlled by operads and besides that, in many cases of interest,
\sT
-cohomology gives an approximation to Quillen cohomology, according to the Quillen principle mentioned earlier. Moreover, as we have seen, it produces a proper cohomology theory not only for operads but also for algebras over them.

Organization of the paper.
In
\lx@sectionsign
2
, we recall briefly some necessary facts relevant to enriched operads and various types of modules over an operad. This section is also devoted to the most important concepts in the paper including tangent category, cotangent complex and Quillen cohomology groups. In
\lx@sectionsign
3
, we set up several conditions on the base category, which we work with throughout
\lx@sectionsign
4
and
\lx@sectionsign
5
. Section
4
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.2.2
. In
\lx@sectionsign
5
, we first set up an extra condition on the base category and by the way, provide several illustrations for this condition. The ultimate goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.2.4
. Section
6
is devoted to Quillen cohomology of simplicial operads. We shall discuss the construction of twisted arrow
\infty
-categories of simplicial operads, after having described the unstraightening of simplicial (co)presheaves. We then explain how this construction classifies Quillen cohomology of simplicial operads.

Acknowledgements.
This paper is part of the author’s PhD thesis, written under the supervision of Yonatan Harpaz at Université Sorbonne Paris Nord. The author is greatly indebted to his advisor for suggesting the problems and for carefully reading earlier drafts of the paper. The author also extends gratitude to the anonymous referees for their valuable comments and suggestions, which have greatly contributed to improving the paper. Special thanks to one referee for providing an idea that allowed the author to significantly improve the computation of
\Tw(\E_{n})
. The author acknowledges support
from the French project ANR-16-CE40-0003 ChroK.

2
Background and notations

This section is devoted to the basic concepts and notations we work with throughout the paper. We first recall briefly fundamental notions relevant to enriched operads, various types of modules over an operad and their homotopy theories. The last subsection is devoted to the needed concepts relevant to the Quillen cohomology theory.

2.1
Enriched operads

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a
symmetric monoidal category
. Given a set
C
, regarded as the set of
colors
, we denote by

\Seq(C):=\{(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\,|\,c_{i},c\in C,n\geqslant 0\}

and refer to it as the collection of
C
-
sequences
. For each
n\geq 0
, we let
\Sigma_{n}
denote the
symmetric group on
n
elements
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

The data of a
symmetric

C
-
collection
(also called a
C
-
symmetric sequence
) in
\mathcal{S}
consist of a collection

M=\{M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\}_{(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\in\Seq(C)}

of objects in
\mathcal{S}
and such that for each
(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\in\Seq(C)
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
, there is a map of the form

\sigma^{*}:M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\longrightarrow M(c_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,c_{\sigma(n)};c).

These maps define a right action by
\Sigma_{n}
in the sense that, for every
\sigma,\tau\in\Sigma_{n}
, we have
\sigma^{*}\tau^{*}=(\tau\sigma)^{*}
and
\iota_{n}^{*}=\Id
where
\iota_{n}\in\Sigma_{n}
signifies the trivial permutation. With the obvious maps, symmetric
C
-collections in
\mathcal{S}
form a category, denoted by
\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})
.

Remark 2.1.2
.

Let
P(C)
denote the groupoid whose set of objects is given by

\Ob(P(C))=\{\emptyset\}\sqcup(\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}C^{\times n})

and whose morphisms consist of the identity
\Id_{\emptyset}
and the morphisms of the form

\sigma^{*}:(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n})\longrightarrow(c_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,c_{\sigma(n)})

with
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
,
n\geqslant 1
. One can then show that the category
\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})
is isomorphic to
\Fun(P(C)\times C,\mathcal{S})
the category of
\mathcal{S}
-enriched functors from
P(C)\times C
to
\mathcal{S}
.

The well known
composite product

-\circ-:\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})\times\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})

endows
\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})
with a monoidal structure. The monoidal unit will be denoted by
\mathcal{I}_{C}
, where
\mathcal{I}_{C}(c;c)=1_{\mathcal{S}}
for every
c\in C
and
\mathcal{I}_{C}
takes the value
\emptyset_{\mathcal{S}}
otherwise. (See, e.g.,
[
23
,
41
,
55
]
).

Definition 2.1.3
.

A
symmetric

C
-
colored operad
in
\mathcal{S}
is a monoid in the monoidal category
(\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S}),-\circ-,\mathcal{I}_{C})
. We denote by
\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
the category of symmetric
C
-colored operads.

Remark 2.1.4
.

Unwinding definition, a symmetric
C
-colored operad in
\mathcal{S}
is a symmetric
C
-collection
\mathcal{P}
equipped with

\bullet
a
composition
whose data consist of the
\Sigma_{*}
-equivariant maps of the form

\mathcal{P}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{1,1},\cdots,c_{1,k_{1}};c_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{n,1},\cdots,c_{{n,k_{n}}};c_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(c_{1,1},\cdots,c_{1,k_{1}},\cdots,c_{n,1},\cdots,c_{{n,k_{n}}};c),

\bullet
and for each color
c\in C
, a
unit operation

\id_{c}:1_{\mathcal{S}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(c;c)
.

The composition must satisfy the essential axioms of associativity and unitality.

Given
\mathcal{P}\in\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
, each object
\mathcal{P}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)
will be called a
space of
n
-ary operations
. Note that the collection of
1
-ary operations of
\mathcal{P}
, denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, inherits an obvious
\mathcal{S}
-enriched category structure. We shall refer to
\mathcal{P}_{1}
as the
underlying category of

\mathcal{P}
.

The notion of a
nonsymmetric
C
-colored operad
(resp.
nonsymmetric
C
-collection
) is the same as that of a symmetric
C
-colored operad (resp. symmetric
C
-collection) without the action of symmetric groups. We denote by
\nsColl_{C}(\mathcal{S})
(resp.
\nsOp_{C}(\mathcal{S})
) the category of nonsymmetric
C
-collections (resp. nonsymmetric
C
-colored operads) in
\mathcal{S}
. The natural passage from nonsymmetric to symmetric context is performed by the
symmetrization functor

\Sym
. Explicitly, the functor
\Sym:\nsColl_{C}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})
is given by sending each nonsymmetric
C
-collection
M
to
\Sym(M)
with

\Sym(M)(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)=\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}}M(c_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,c_{\sigma(n)};c).

The structure maps are induced by the multiplication of permutations in an evident way. By construction, the functor
\Sym
forms a left adjoint to the associated forgetful functor.

Remark 2.1.5
.

Given two objects
M,N\in\nsColl_{C}(\mathcal{S})
, there is an obvious natural inclusion in
\nsColl_{C}(\mathcal{S})
of the form

M\,\overline{\circ}\,N\longrightarrow\Sym(M)\circ\Sym(N)

where
-\overline{\circ}-
refers to the composite product of nonsymmetric
C
-collections. This induces a natural map
\Sym(M\,{\overline{\circ}}\,N)\longrightarrow\Sym(M)\circ\Sym(N)
. It can then be verified that the latter is a natural isomorphism of symmetric
C
-collections with the inverse

\Sym(M)\circ\Sym(N)\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Sym(M\,{\overline{\circ}}\,N)

being induced by the concatenation of linear orders

\Sigma_{k}\times\Sigma_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n_{k}}\longrightarrow\Sigma_{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}}.

Moreover, it is easy to see that the functor
\Sym
preserves monoidal units and thus, it is strong monoidal.

By the above remark, the functor
\Sym:\nsColl_{C}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})
descends to a functor between monoids

\Sym:\nsOp_{C}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S}),

which forms a left adjoint to the associated forgetful functor.

Remark 2.1.6
.

Since we are mostly interested in the symmetric context, from now on, unless
otherwise specified, we shall omit the word
``
symmetric” when mentioning an object of
\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
(or
\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})
).

One can integrate all the categories
\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
for
C\in\Sets
into a single category of
\mathcal{S}
-enriched operads, just like the way one establishes the category of categories.

Definition 2.1.7
.

The
category of

\mathcal{S}
-
enriched operads
, denoted by
\Op(\mathcal{S})
, is the (contravariant)
Grothendieck construction

\Op(\mathcal{S}):=\int_{C\in\Sets}\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})

in which for each map
\alpha:C\rightarrow D
of sets, the corresponding functor
\alpha^{*}:\Op_{D}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
is given by taking
\mathcal{Q}\in\Op_{D}(\mathcal{S})
to
\alpha^{*}\mathcal{Q}
with

\alpha^{*}\mathcal{Q}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c):=\mathcal{Q}(\alpha(c_{1}),\cdots,\alpha(c_{n});\alpha(c)).

Unwinding definition, an object of
\Op(\mathcal{S})
is a pair
(C,\mathcal{P})
with
C\in\Sets
and
\mathcal{P}\in\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
, while a morphism
(C,\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow(D,\mathcal{Q})
consists of a map
\alpha:C\rightarrow D
of sets and a map
f:\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\alpha^{*}\mathcal{Q}
of
C
-colored operads.

Remark 2.1.8
.

For
\mathcal{P}\in\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
, there is an adjunction

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{P}}:\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})_{\mathcal{P}/}\mathrel{\vbox{\halign{\hfil$#$\hfil\cr\scriptstyle\cr\kern 0.43057pt\cr\relbar\joinrel\rightharpoonup\cr\kern-1.29167pt\cr\begin{picture}\roundcap\polyline(0,0)(1,0)
\polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
\end{picture}\cr\leftharpoondown\joinrel\relbar\cr\scriptstyle\cr}}}\Op(\mathcal{S})_{\mathcal{P}/}:\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{P}}

(2.1.1)

where the left adjoint
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{P}}
is the obvious embedding functor. Let
\mathcal{P}\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{Q}
be an object of
\Op(\mathcal{S})_{\mathcal{P}/}
. Then
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{Q})
is given on each level by the restriction of colors:

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{Q})(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c):=\mathcal{Q}(f(c_{1}),\cdots,f(c_{n});f(c)).

2.2
Various types of operadic modules

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a
C
-colored operad in
\mathcal{S}
. We let
\LMod(\mathcal{P})
(resp.
\RMod(\mathcal{P})
) denote the category of
left (resp. right)

\mathcal{P}
-
modules
. Moreover, we let
\BMod(\mathcal{P})
and
\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
respectively denote the categories of
\mathcal{P}
-
bimodules
and
infinitesimal

\mathcal{P}
-
bimodules
. Let us revisit these quickly.

Operadic left module (resp. right module, bimodule) is the usual notion of left module (resp. right module, bimodule) over an operad when one regards operads as monoids in the monoidal category of symmetric sequences. More explicitly, these are expressed as follows.

Definition 2.2.1
.

(i) A
left
\mathcal{P}
-module
is a
C
-collection
M
equipped with a
left

\mathcal{P}
-
action
map
\mathcal{P}\circ M\longrightarrow M
whose data consist of the
\Sigma_{*}
-equivariant maps of the form

\mathcal{P}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes M(d_{1,1},\cdots,d_{1,k_{1}};c_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes M(d_{n,1},\cdots,d_{n,k_{n}};c_{n})\longrightarrow M(d_{1,1},\cdots,d_{1,k_{1}},\cdots,d_{n,1},\cdots,d_{n,k_{n}};c)

satisfying the classical axioms of associativity and unitality for left modules.

(ii) Dually, a
right
\mathcal{P}
-module
is a
C
-collection
M
equipped with a
right

\mathcal{P}
-
action
map
M\circ\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow M
whose data consist of the
\Sigma_{*}
-equivariant maps of the form

M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes\mathcal{P}(d_{1,1},\cdots,d_{1,k_{1}};c_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}(d_{n,1},\cdots,d_{n,k_{n}};c_{n})\longrightarrow M(d_{1,1},\cdots,d_{1,k_{1}},\cdots,d_{n,1},\cdots,d_{n,k_{n}};c)

satisfying the classical axioms of associativity and unitality for right modules.

(iii) A
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule
is a
C
-collection
M
equipped with both a left and a right
\mathcal{P}
-module structure. These must satisfy the essential axiom of compatibility.

Moreover, a
\mathcal{P}
-algebra is simply a left
\mathcal{P}
-module concentrated in level 0. More precisely, this is expressed as follows.

Definition 2.2.2
.

A
\mathcal{P}
-
algebra
is an object
A\in\mathcal{S}^{\times\,C}
which is equipped, for each
(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)
, with a
\mathcal{P}
-action map

\mathcal{P}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes A(c_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes A(c_{n})\longrightarrow A(c).

These maps must satisfy the essential axioms of associativity, unitality and equivariance. We denote by
\Alg_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{S})
the category of
\mathcal{P}
-algebras.

Remark 2.2.3
.

When
\mathcal{P}
is concentrated in arity 1 then
\mathcal{P}
is simply an
\mathcal{S}
-enriched category. In this situation, the category of
\mathcal{P}
-algebras coincides with
\Fun(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{S})
the category of
\mathcal{S}
-valued enriched functors on
\mathcal{P}
.

Example 2.2.4
.

The collection of nullary (= 0-ary) operations of
\mathcal{P}
, denoted
\mathcal{P}_{0}
, inherits an obvious
\mathcal{P}
-algebra structure and moreover,
\mathcal{P}_{0}
then becomes an initial object in
\Alg_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{S})
.

Example 2.2.5
.

It is noteworthy that for each set
C
, there exists a
\Seq(C)
-colored operad in
\mathcal{S}
, denoted by
\textbf{O}_{C}
, whose algebras are precisely the
C
-colored operads, i.e., there is an isomorphism of categories
\Alg_{\textbf{O}_{C}}(\mathcal{S})\cong\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
. We will refer to
\textbf{O}_{C}
as the
operad of
C
-colored operads
. (Cf., e.g., [
[
21
]
, §3]).

The structure of left modules (and hence, bimodules) over an operad is not linear in general. The notion of
infinitesimal left modules (bimodules)
, as introduced by Merkulov-Vallette (
[
39
]
), essentially appears as the linearization of the previous one. In terms of enriched colored operads, these are defined as follows.

Definition 2.2.6
.

1.

An
infinitesimal left
\mathcal{P}
-module
is a
C
-collection
M
equipped with the action maps of the form

\circ^{l}_{i}:\mathcal{P}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes M(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};c_{i})\longrightarrow M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{i-1},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m},c_{i+1},\cdots,c_{n};c)

which are
\Sigma_{*}
-equivariant and satisfy the classical axioms of associativity and unitality for left modules.

2.

Dually, an
infinitesimal right
\mathcal{P}
-module
is a
C
-collection
M
equipped with the action maps of the form

\circ^{r}_{i}:M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes\mathcal{P}(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};c_{i})\longrightarrow M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{i-1},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m},c_{i+1},\cdots,c_{n};c)

which are
\Sigma_{*}
-equivariant and satisfy the classical axioms of associativity and unitality for right modules.

3.

An
infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule
is a
C
-collection
M
equipped with both an infinitesimal left and an infinitesimal right
\mathcal{P}
-module structure which together satisfy the essential compatibility.

Remark 2.2.7
.

The structure of an infinitesimal right
\mathcal{P}
-module is equivalent to that of a (non-infinitesimal) right
\mathcal{P}
-module.

The definition of an infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule can be reformulated using the diagramatical language, which has certain advantages over the above definition. To this end, one starts with the notion of
infinitesimal composite product
:

-\circ_{(1)}-:\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})\times\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S}),

which can be thought of as the
``
right linearization” of the composite product
-\circ-
. Formally, given two
C
-collections
M
and
N
, the
C
-collection
M\circ_{(1)}N
is given by the maximal sub-collection of
M\circ(\mathcal{I}_{C}\sqcup N)
which is linear in
N
. To be precise, looking at the explicit formula of
M\circ(\mathcal{I}_{C}\sqcup N)
, we have on each level that
(M\circ_{(1)}N)(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)
is the sub-object of
M\circ(\mathcal{I}_{C}\sqcup N)(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)
consisting of the
multi-tensor products
which contain one and only one factor in
N
. The readers can find this construction in [
[
34
]
, Section 6.1]. Observe now that for each
M\in\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})
there is a natural inclusion

\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}(\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}M)\longrightarrow(\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}\mathcal{P})\circ_{(1)}M.

On other hand, the (partial) composition in
\mathcal{P}
gives a map
\mu_{(1)}:\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
. The following is equivalent to Definition
2.2.6
(i).

Definition 2.2.8
.

An
infinitesimal left

\mathcal{P}
-
module
is a
C
-collection
M
equipped with an action map
\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}M\longrightarrow M
satisfying the classical axioms of associativity and unitality for left modules, which are depicted as the commutativity of the following diagrams

\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}(\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}M)
(\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}\mathcal{P})\circ_{(1)}M
\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}M
M
\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}M
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{C}\circ_{(1)}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Now notice that for each
M\in\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})
there is a natural inclusion

(\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}M)\circ\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow(\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P})\circ_{(1)}(M\circ\mathcal{P}).

The following is equivalent to Definition
2.2.6
(iii).

Definition 2.2.9
.

An infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule is a
C
-collection
M
endowed with an infinitesimal left
\mathcal{P}
-module structure, exhibited by a map
\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}M\longrightarrow M
and with a right
\mathcal{P}
-module structure, exhibited by a map
M\circ\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow M
. These are subject to the axiom of compatibility, depicted as the commutativity of the following diagram

(\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}M)\circ\mathcal{P}
(\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P})\circ_{(1)}(M\circ\mathcal{P})
\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}M
M
M\circ\mathcal{P}

Remark 2.2.10
.

In the above diagram, the
C
-collection
(\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}M)\circ\mathcal{P}
does not represent the free infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule generated by
M
. (This does not even carry a canonical infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule structure). To find the exact one, we first factor the free functor
\Free^{ib}:\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
as

\Coll_{C}(\mathcal{S})\overset{\mathcal{F}_{1}}{\longrightarrow}\RMod(\mathcal{P})\overset{\mathcal{F}_{2}}{\longrightarrow}\IbMod(\mathcal{P})

where
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
are the left adjoints to the associated forgetful functors. It can then be shown that
\mathcal{F}_{1}\cong(-)\circ\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}\cong\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}(-)
. In conclusion, the functor
\Free^{ib}
is given by the formula

\Free^{ib}=\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}(-\circ\mathcal{P}).

Remark 2.2.11
.

Note that the map
\mu_{(1)}:\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
endows
\mathcal{P}
with the structure of an infinitesimal bimodule over itself. Now let
M
be a
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule under
\mathcal{P}
. Then,
M
inherits a canonical infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule structure (under
\mathcal{P}
) induced by inserting the unit operations of
\mathcal{P}
into
M
. This procedure determines a
restriction functor

\BMod(\mathcal{P})_{\mathcal{P}/}\longrightarrow\IbMod(\mathcal{P})_{\mathcal{P}/},

(2.2.1)

which admits a left adjoint named
induction functor
.

Another important one is the notion of
modules over an operadic algebra
. Let
A
be a
\mathcal{P}
-algebra.

Definition 2.2.12
.

An
A
-module over

\mathcal{P}
is an object
M\in\mathcal{S}^{\times\,C}
equipped, for each sequence
(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)
, with a mixed
(\mathcal{P},A)
-action map of the form

\mathcal{P}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes\;\bigotimes_{i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}-\{k\}}\;A(c_{i})\otimes M(c_{k})\longrightarrow M(c).

These maps must satisfy the essential axioms of associativity, unitality and equivariance. With the obvious maps,
A
-modules over
\mathcal{P}
form a category, denoted by
\Mod_{\mathcal{P}}^{A}
.

To reformulate
\Mod_{\mathcal{P}}^{A}
as a category of
\mathcal{S}
-valued enriched functors, one will need the construction of
enveloping operads
. Denote by
\Pairs_{C}(\mathcal{S})
the category whose objects are the pairs
(\mathcal{P},A)
with
\mathcal{P}\in\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
and
A\in\Alg_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{S})
, and whose morphisms are the pairs
(\varphi,f):(\mathcal{P},A)\longrightarrow(\mathcal{Q},B)
with
\varphi:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}
being a map in
\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
and
f:A\rightarrow B
a map of
\mathcal{P}
-algebras. There is a canonical functor
\delta:\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\Pairs_{C}(\mathcal{S})
sending each
C
-colored operad
\mathcal{P}
to the pair
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}_{0})
(see Example
2.2.4
). According to
[
6
]
, the functor
\delta
admits a left adjoint
\Env:\Pairs_{C}(\mathcal{S})\longrightarrow\Op_{C}(\mathcal{S})
called the
enveloping functor
.

Definition 2.2.13
.

The
enveloping operad
associated to a pair
(\mathcal{P},A)\in\Pairs_{C}(\mathcal{S})
is defined to be
\Env(\mathcal{P},A)
the image of
(\mathcal{P},A)
through the enveloping functor.

Remark 2.2.14
.

According to [Theorem 1.10,
[
6
]
], there is a canonical isomorphism

\Mod_{\mathcal{P}}^{A}\cong\Fun(\Env(\mathcal{P},A)_{1},\mathcal{S})

(2.2.2)

between the category of
A
-modules over
\mathcal{P}
and the category of
\mathcal{S}
-valued enriched functors on
\Env(\mathcal{P},A)_{1}
the underlying category of
\Env(\mathcal{P},A)
.

Remark 2.2.15
.

Another main interest in this construction is that there is a canonical isomorphism

\Alg_{\Env(\mathcal{P},A)}(\mathcal{S})\cong\Alg_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{S})_{A/}

between the categories of
\Env(\mathcal{P},A)
-algebras and
\mathcal{P}
-algebras under
A
. Furthermore, by construction there is a canonical map
j_{A}:\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\Env(\mathcal{P},A)
of
C
-colored operads. This endows
\Env(\mathcal{P},A)_{0}
with a canonical
\mathcal{P}
-algebra structure and moreover,
\Env(\mathcal{P},A)_{0}
is isomorphic to
A
as
\mathcal{P}
-algebras. (See around [
[
6
]
, Lemma 1.7]).

It is convenient that each of the categories
\IbMod(\mathcal{P})
,
\BMod(\mathcal{P})
,
\LMod(\mathcal{P})
and
\RMod(\mathcal{P})
can be encoded by an enriched operad (or category). In terms of single-colored operads, these constructions can be found in
[
1
,
11
]
.

Notations 2.2.16
.

1.

We let
\Fin
denote the smallest skeleton of the category of finite sets whose objects consist of
\underline{0}:=\emptyset
and
\underline{m}:=\{1,\cdots,m\}
for
m\geqslant 1
.

2.

We denote by
\Fin_{*}
the category whose objects are finite pointed sets
\left\langle m\right\rangle:=\{0,1,\cdots,m\}
(with
0
as the basepoint) for
m\geqslant 0
, and whose morphisms are basepoint-preserving maps. In other words,
\Fin_{*}
is the smallest skeleton of the category of finite pointed sets.

Note that there is an obvious embedding functor
\Fin\longrightarrow\Fin_{*}
taking each
\underline{m}
to
\langle m\rangle
.

Construction 2.2.17
.

We now construct an
\mathcal{S}
-enriched category, denoted
\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}
, which encodes infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodules. The set of objects of
\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}
is
\Seq(C)
, while its mapping spaces are defined as follows. For each map
\left\langle m\right\rangle\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}\left\langle n\right\rangle
in
\Fin_{*}
, we denote by

\Map^{f}_{\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),\left(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d\right)\,\right):=\mathcal{P}\left(c,\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(0)};d\right)\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1,\cdots,n}\mathcal{P}\left(\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(i)};c_{i}\right)

in which, for each
i\in\{0,\cdots,n\}
, the elements of
\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(i)}
are put in the natural ascending order of
j
. Then, we define

\Map_{\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),\left(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d\right)\,\right):=\bigsqcup_{\left\langle m\right\rangle\overset{f}{\rightarrow}\left\langle n\right\rangle}\Map^{f}_{\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),\left(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d\right)\,\right).

Observe that

\Map^{\Id_{\left\langle n\right\rangle}}_{\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right)\,\right)=\mathcal{P}(c;c)\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{1};c_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{n};c_{n}).

Due to this, we can define the unit morphisms of
\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}
via the unit operations of
\mathcal{P}
. Moreover, the structure maps of
\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}
are canonically defined via the composition in
\mathcal{P}
, along with the symmetric action on
\mathcal{P}
. (See also
[
1
]
,
\lx@sectionsign
2).

Proposition 2.2.18
.

There is a canonical isomorphism

\IbMod(\mathcal{P})\cong\Fun(\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{S})

between the category of infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodules and the category of
\mathcal{S}
-valued enriched functors on
\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}
.

Sketch of proof.

(1) Let
M:\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}
be an enriched functor. We construct the associated infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule, still denoted
M
, as follows. Each permutation
\alpha\in\Sigma_{n}
determines a map
\left\langle n\right\rangle\overset{\alpha}{\longrightarrow}\left\langle n\right\rangle
. Observe now that

\Map^{\alpha}_{\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),(c_{\alpha(1)},\cdots,c_{\alpha(n)};c)\,\right)=\mathcal{P}(c;c)\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{1};c_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{n};c_{n}).

In particular, the functor structure map of
M

\Map_{\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),(c_{\alpha(1)},\cdots,c_{\alpha(n)};c)\,\right)\otimes M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\longrightarrow M(c_{\alpha(1)},\cdots,c_{\alpha(n)};c)

has a component of the form

\mathcal{P}(c;c)\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{1};c_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{n};c_{n})\otimes M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\longrightarrow M(c_{\alpha(1)},\cdots,c_{\alpha(n)};c).

Now, the evaluation at the unit operations
\id_{c},\id_{c_{1}},\cdots,\id_{c_{n}}
of
\mathcal{P}
determines an action map of the form
M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\overset{\alpha^{*}}{\longrightarrow}M(c_{\alpha(1)},\cdots,c_{\alpha(n)};c)
.

Next we define the infinitesimal right action of
\mathcal{P}
on
M
. Observe that the structure map

\Map_{\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),(c_{1},\cdots,c_{i-1},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m},c_{i+1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\,\right)\otimes M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)

\longrightarrow M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{i-1},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m},c_{i+1},\cdots,c_{n};c)

has a component of the form

M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes\mathcal{P}(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};c_{i})\otimes\mathcal{P}(c;c)\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{1};c_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{i-1};c_{i-1})\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{i+1};c_{i+1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{n};c_{n})

\longrightarrow M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{i-1},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m},c_{i+1},\cdots,c_{n};c).

This induces an infinitesimal right
\mathcal{P}
-action on
M
by evaluating at the unit operations of
\mathcal{P}
.

To define the infinitesimal left
\mathcal{P}
-action, observe that the structure map

\Map_{\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};c_{i}\right),(c_{1},\cdots,c_{i-1},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m},c_{i+1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\,\right)\otimes M(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};c_{i})

\longrightarrow M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{i-1},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m},c_{i+1},\cdots,c_{n};c)

has a component of the form

\mathcal{P}(c_{i},c_{1},\cdots,c_{i-1},c_{i+1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes\mathcal{P}(d_{1};d_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}(d_{m};d_{m})\otimes M(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};c_{i})

\longrightarrow M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{i-1},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m},c_{i+1},\cdots,c_{n};c).

The latter induces an infinitesimal left action by evaluating at the unit operations of
\mathcal{P}
, again.

(2) Conversely, let
M
be an infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule. We need to endow
M
with a canonical enriched functor structure
\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}
. To this end, we have to define the maps of the form

\Map_{\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d)\,\right)\otimes M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\longrightarrow M(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d).

This map must consist of, for each
\left\langle m\right\rangle\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}\left\langle n\right\rangle
, a component map of the form

\mathcal{P}\left(c,\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(0)};d\right)\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1,\cdots,n}\mathcal{P}\left(\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(i)};c_{i}\right)\otimes M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\longrightarrow M(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d).

The latter can be naturally defined using the (two sided) infinitesimal
\mathcal{P}
-action on
M
, along with the action of the symmetric groups on
M
.
∎

Construction 2.2.19
.

The enriched category which encodes the category of right
\mathcal{P}
-modules will be denoted by
\textbf{R}^{\mathcal{P}}
. Its set of objects is
\Seq(C)
, while its mapping objects are given by

\Map_{\textbf{R}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),\left(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};c\right)\,\right):=\bigsqcup_{\underline{m}\overset{f}{\rightarrow}\underline{n}}\left[\bigotimes_{i=1,\cdots,n}\mathcal{P}\left(\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(i)};c_{i}\right)\right]

where the coproduct ranges over the hom-set
\textrm{Hom}_{\Fin}(\underline{m},\underline{n})
. Observe that there is a map

\Map_{\textbf{R}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),\left(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};c\right)\,\right)\longrightarrow\Map_{\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};c)\,\right)

induced by the embedding
\Fin\longrightarrow\Fin_{*}
and by inserting the unit operation
\id_{c}
into the factor
\mathcal{P}(c;c)
of the right hand side. The categorical structure of
\textbf{R}^{\mathcal{P}}
is then defined via the operad structure of
\mathcal{P}
, so that
\textbf{R}^{\mathcal{P}}
forms a subcategory of
\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}
.

One can then prove the following, similarly as in the proof of Proposition
2.2.18
.

Proposition 2.2.20
.

There is a canonical isomorphism

\RMod(\mathcal{P})\cong\Fun(\textbf{R}^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{S})

between the category of right
\mathcal{P}
-modules and the category of
\mathcal{S}
-valued enriched functors on
\textbf{R}^{\mathcal{P}}
.

We now wish to construct an operad encoding the category of
\mathcal{P}
-bimodules, yet it will be more convenient for us to start with
\BMod(\mathcal{P})_{\mathcal{P}/}
the category of
\mathcal{P}
-bimodules under
\mathcal{P}
.

Construction 2.2.21
.

The
\mathcal{S}
-enriched operad which encodes
\mathcal{P}
-bimodules under
\mathcal{P}
will be denoted by
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}
. Its set of colors is again
\Seq(C)
. The nullary operations of
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}
agree with
\mathcal{P}
, i.e.,

\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}(;(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)):=\mathcal{P}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c),

while its 1-ary operations coincide with those of
\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}
(see Construction
2.2.17
), i.e.,

\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right);\left(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d\right)\,\right)=\bigsqcup_{\left\langle m\right\rangle\overset{f}{\rightarrow}\left\langle n\right\rangle}\left[\mathcal{P}\left(c,\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(0)};d\right)\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1,\cdots,n}\mathcal{P}\left(\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(i)};c_{i}\right)\right]

where
f
ranges over the set
\textrm{Hom}_{\Fin_{*}}(\left\langle m\right\rangle,\left\langle n\right\rangle)
. Then we may extend the above formula to obtain the spaces of operations of higher arities. A typical space of
n
-ary operations of
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}
is given by

\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{r_{1}};c^{(1)}\right),\left(c_{r_{1}+1},\cdots,c_{r_{1}+r_{2}};c^{(2)}\right),\cdots,\left(c_{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n-1}+1},\cdots,c_{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}};c^{(n)}\right);\left(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d\right)\,\right)

=\bigsqcup_{\left\langle m\right\rangle\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}\left\langle r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}\right\rangle}\left[\mathcal{P}\left(c^{(1)},\cdots,c^{(n)},\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(0)};d\right)\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1,\cdots,r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}}\mathcal{P}\left(\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(i)};c_{i}\right)\right].

The
\Sigma_{n}
-action is given by permuting the colors
c^{(1)},\cdots,c^{(n)}
on the factor

\mathcal{P}\left(c^{(1)},\cdots,c^{(n)},\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(0)};d\right)

and simultaneously, permuting the terms
\{c_{1},\cdots,c_{r_{1}}\},\cdots,\{c_{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n-1}+1},\cdots,c_{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}}\}
on the factor

\bigotimes_{i=1,\cdots,r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}}\mathcal{P}\left(\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(i)};c_{i}\right).

The composition in
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}
is canonically defined via the composition in
\mathcal{P}
, while its unit operations are exactly those of
\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}
.

Proposition 2.2.22
.

There is a canonical isomorphism

\BMod(\mathcal{P})_{\mathcal{P}/}\cong\Alg_{\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}}(\mathcal{S})

between the category of
\mathcal{P}
-bimodules under
\mathcal{P}
and the category of algebras over
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}
.

Sketch of proof.

(1) Let
M
be a
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}
-algebra. Note first that since the underlying category of
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}
agrees with
\textbf{Ib}^{\mathcal{P}}
,
M
already inherits a canonical right
\mathcal{P}
-module structure (cf. Proposition
2.2.18
).

Let us see how
M
comes equipped with a left
\mathcal{P}
-action. For simplicity, we describe only the action maps of the form

\mathcal{P}(c,d;e)\otimes M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes M(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d)\longrightarrow M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};e)

(2.2.3)

To this end, observe first that the
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}
-algebra structure map of
M
of the form

\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c\right),\left(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d\right);\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};e\right)\,\right)\otimes

\otimes\;M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes M(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d)\longrightarrow M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};e)

has a component of the form

\mathcal{P}(c,d;e)\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{1};c_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}(c_{n};c_{n})\otimes\mathcal{P}(d_{1};d_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{P}(d_{m};d_{m})\otimes

\otimes\;M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n};c)\otimes M(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d)\longrightarrow M(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n},d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};e).

The evaluation at the unit operations
\id_{c_{1}},\cdots,\id_{c_{n}},\id_{d_{1}},\cdots,\id_{d_{m}}
of
\mathcal{P}
to the latter gives us the action map (
2.2.3
) as desired.

Moreover, the action of the nullary operations of
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}
on
M
determines a canonical map
\mathcal{P}\rightarrow M
.

(2) Conversely, let
M
be a
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule under
\mathcal{P}
. We let the composed map

[\mathcal{I}_{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow M]=:\varepsilon

exhibit the images of the unit operations of
\mathcal{P}
in
M
. In order to construct a
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}/}
-algebra structure on
M
, one will need to make use of the
\mathcal{P}
-bimodule structure of
M
and the map
\varepsilon
in a suitable way.

∎

Construction 2.2.23
.

The
\mathcal{S}
-enriched operad which encodes the category of
\mathcal{P}
-bimodules will be denoted by
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}}
. Its set of colors is again
\Seq(C)
. A typical space of
n
-ary operations is given by

\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}}\left(\,\left(c_{1},\cdots,c_{r_{1}};c^{(1)}\right),\left(c_{r_{1}+1},\cdots,c_{r_{1}+r_{2}};c^{(2)}\right),\cdots,\left(c_{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n-1}+1},\cdots,c_{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}};c^{(n)}\right);\left(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m};d\right)\,\right)

:=\bigsqcup_{\underline{m}\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}\underline{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}}}\left[\mathcal{P}\left(c^{(1)},\cdots,c^{(n)};d\right)\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1,\cdots,r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}}\mathcal{P}\left(\{d_{j}\}_{j\in f^{-1}(i)};c_{i}\right)\right]

where the coproduct ranges over the hom-set
\textrm{Hom}_{\Fin}(\underline{m},\underline{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}})
. For each map
f:\underline{m}\longrightarrow\underline{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}}
, we will denote by
\textbf{B}_{f}^{\mathcal{P}}(-)
the component of
\textbf{B}^{\mathcal{P}}(-)
corresponding to
f
